Cast A Blind Shadow
A Tale of Homicides and Hauntings
By
Bill Gill
________________
Chapter 1


        The idea of a building holding the energy of its past is not so far-fetched. We all see the memories of times spent in places, evidence that people have used or experienced a space before us. A couch that maintains the impression of where the former owner sat. Handrails on stairs that are worn smooth from the touch of thousands of hands. A floor that exhibits a habitual path taken by those who trod the wood before you. These all have energy soaked into the fiber of the materials from which they were constructed. Energy that came from human spirits. Is it possible that those spirits are still connected to that energy? Might they not make themselves known at times? Could they not still be walking among us? Patty Robertson believed they could.
Patty started the Central States Paranormal Group (CSPG) in order to provide paranormal investigation experiences for a growing number of interested amateurs as well as seasoned pros. The group pooled its resources to purchase several properties around the Midwest, noted for having unexplained activities. She had just arrived at Mt. Hope Corners, Wisconsin, a small crossroads outside of Eau Claire. Standing on a large section of land was the once grand Lockwood Sanatorium. Patty was meeting the current owner, Harvey Pool. 
“Hello, you must be Harvey.”
“That is correct. Welcome to the Lockwood.”
Harvey was a local historian who believed in the preservation of the complex. 
“I can tell you are very proud of the place. I was surprised when the realtor said you needed to meet in person. Why do you want to sell?”
“To be honest, I don’t want to sell, but unfortunately, I did not have the resources to upgrade the facility. The property taxes alone are hurting me financially. I suppose that isn’t a good negotiation strategy, letting you know my position, but I just want the place in good hands. I see the CSPG as an opportunity to get the place to someone who will preserve the property as it is and keep it from just rotting away into the woods. I wanted to meet you in person and explain my position. I also wanted to hear directly from you that you plan to maintain the place. The realtor and your legal people can handle the paperwork. I need to have your promise and a handshake.”
“I respect that. I want you to know that our goal will not be to restore the place. We will work toward making it weather-tight eventually, and safe for people to walk through, but it will remain outdated and well … creepy.”
“I understand that. I just want it to be standing so that people will know its history. Too many of our past achievements are forgotten as new and shiny modern developments are built on land that has seen so much of our story. A people without history are a people with no roots.”
“I can tell history is important to you. It plays a big part in what we do with these buildings as well. Could you fill me in on some of that history? We will be putting together a brochure about the place and, as I said, the history is important. Now, once again, I want to be up front about the fact that we will focus on deaths that occurred in the building. Our clientele wants to know about the possible spectral inhabitants.”
“That makes sense. I don’t personally believe in ghosts, but if that is what people want to know, we have had our share of deaths, starting with Mrs. Lockwood herself.”
“I would love to hear that story.”
“I need to start at the beginning if you don’t mind.”
“By all means.”
Patty was willing to hear the whole tale if it meant the sale would go through at her price.
“On April 19th, 1901, the Lockwood Hospital was established on one hundred acres donated by Josiah Lockwood. Members of the Lockwood family, the Mayor of Eau Claire, and the leading citizens of Rock Falls, Carryville, and Mt. Hope Corners attended the groundbreaking. These men were the primary contributors to the construction cost. The building was built on a timber frame and surfaced with stones gathered from the river beds of the Chippewa and Eau Claire Rivers. At the time, the building was the model of the current medical science of the day. It had four doctors in residence and boasted a staff of thirty nurses and orderlies.”
“Now that was just the center building as we look at it today, correct?”
“Yes, you can see the wings are built with grey brick to match the limestone of the center building. The project had been the passion of Josiah’s wife, Mary Lockwood. She was born in the western part of Eau Claire County on a large dairy farm, which she inherited. It was her wish that a portion of the farm be dedicated to the health of the community. The Lockwood family had been in lumbering since the mid eighteen hundreds, and they had amassed a fortune in the Eau Claire area. Josiah and his wife provided the land and the lumber to build the three-story health care facility. Its role was to service the rural citizens of the western side of the county, which it did for twenty years.”
“That was when it closed the first time, right? At least that’s what my research turned up.” 
“That’s correct. Unfortunately, during those twenty years, it lost money annually until it had nearly drained the once seemingly limitless funds of the Lockwood family. Josiah died in 1907, leaving Mary to manage the business and the hospital. Seeing her fortune dwindle and the hospital failing was too much for her, and on the fifth of February, 1921, Mary closed the doors of the facility for her last time. She then walked to the second floor, where she overlooked the grand entry foyer and hanged herself in the empty hospital. Some say they could hear her scream from over a mile away.”
“That is a great story! I mean, I guess not for the Lockwood family, but for our people, that is a terrific opening story.”
“Yes, I can believe that. When I tell people the history that always seems to be one of the most interesting parts for them.”
“How did the hospital end up being enlarged? From what I read, it was reopened as a sanatorium around 1925.”
“Well, in 1921, Wisconsin was experiencing the height of the spread of Tuberculosis. In that year, it was the number one cause of death in the state. Nearly three thousand people died of the disease in 1925 alone.”
“Wow”
“The state took the property for back taxes owed. It was still in good condition, having been closed for just a couple of months. The left wing was added first. It soon became obvious that more space was going to be needed, so construction started on the other two wings, and by 1925, the building was as you see it today.”
“There had to be quite a few deaths here during that time.”
“It is difficult to find an exact number, because most sanatoriums were very discreet about deaths. It was a difficult situation. Obviously, they feared they would demoralize the patients in treatment if they allowed them to hear the death toll numbers. As was the case in most sanatoriums around the nation, the number of deaths is most certainly underreported. The official number from 1923 to 1950 was as many as ten thousand deaths.”
“Did I hear that number correctly? Did you say ten thousand?”
“You heard right. That does not take into account the three nurses who jumped from the roof.”
“I haven’t heard about that.”
“They didn’t all jump at the same time, and there is controversy around whether they jumped or were thrown from the roof.”
“This one sounds juicy.”
“In the 1930s, there was a doctor here who fashioned himself as a ladies' man. As the story goes, he was involved with several nurses here at the facility. All three of them were married. That was not a deterrent for Doctor Zuwiki. In fact, he liked the idea that they were not free to pursue him after he broke it off with them. The first had become pregnant. When her husband found out about the affair, he rejected the child and kicked her out of his house. Zuwiki refused to acknowledge the child or support the woman. She climbed the three flights of steps and then the ladder to the roof hatch. She stood on the roof of the central structure, right up there.”
Harvey pointed to the space between the gargoyles directly above the
 front door. 
“She stood there and scandalously bared her chest to the wind and flung herself off onto the front steps. It is said that if you look hard enough, her blood stains can still be seen. The story created quite a scandal. Zuwiki was given a warning for his behavior. That, however, did not slow down his appetite for married women. A year later, two of the nurses found out that they were both the subjects of his false affections.”
“That guy was a real piece of work.”
“Without question, the facts are a little fuzzy, but when the two women fell from the roof in nearly the exact location as the first, Zuwiki was up there with them. He claimed he was there trying to keep them from jumping, but failed. There was no evidence to the contrary, but rumors persisted that he lured them to the roof and threw them off.”
“Wow, he sounds like an evil, egotistical monster. Stories of that type of scandalous behavior actually work very well for our type of business.”
“I can understand that. This will probably work for you as well. It is these three nurses that people have said they see in the windows from outside. I have had people tell me they have seen the nurses walking the hallways.  Perhaps justice was served by fate. Zuwiki ultimately came to a grizzly ending.”
“Was that on the property as well?”
“Yes, he somehow ended up in the boiler room, scalded to death by the pressure relief valve of the boiler. It was a gruesome death, as the skin was boiled off his bones. Some say he still wanders the halls as a shadow figure, seeking revenge for his death.”
“Have you ever seen the shadow figure?”
“I don’t think so. There are a lot of shadows here at all times of the day. And some of them have spooked me. But I can’t say I’ve seen one that steps away from the wall as others have described.”
“So, from 1950 until 1991, the Lockwood was primarily an elder care facility, right?”
“Yes, and of course, during those years, deaths were a pretty regular occurrence. One in particular was very sad. In 1979, Helen Cochran became a resident of the Lockwood Senior Care Center. It was a privately run facility at that time. A group of investors from Milwaukee who owned several elder care locations in Wisconsin and Northern Illinois bought the place in 1976. They ran it as a business until it closed in 1998.”
“Okay, what about Helen Cochran?”
“Oh, I’m sorry, I was telling that story, wasn’t I. She was a woman in her mid-seventies. She should have been ready for retirement, but life events have a way of changing a person’s plans. Helen had one child, a daughter named Martha. Martha was a wild one, often in trouble. When she became pregnant, without the benefit of marriage, Helen pressured her to have the child. Unfortunately, motherhood did not suit Martha. She ran off the first chance she had, leaving Helen to care for the child.”
“What year was that?”
“That was in 1973. It was difficult for Helen. She did not have a career; after her husband died, she started to clean houses for a living. She struggled to make ends meet, but managed to provide for herself and her grandson, Charley. Then the accident took place in 1979. Helen was run over by a car while she was walking home from one of her cleaning jobs. She lost her right leg, and the recuperation was slow. It was difficult to heal from the injury, and then she developed pneumonia. She had no choice but to give up the child and seek admittance for herself in the care facility. The accident provided funds for her care for the rest of her life. The child, a boy, ended up in foster homes until he was adopted by a family in Eau Claire.”
“That is a heart-wrenching story, but you said her death was very sad.”
“It was sad and horrible, though ruled an accident, there were several who suspected otherwise.”
“This has been quite a buildup. What happened?”
“Helen adjusted to life here as best as she could. Her guilt over giving up the boy weighed heavily upon her. She tried to keep busy so as not to dwell on what she viewed as her failures, first her daughter, who died of a drug overdose, and then having to abandon her grandson. She spent most of her time knitting. On the day she died, she was seated in the group room working on her needlecraft to produce a blanket. She made receiving blankets that she gave to the hospital for newborns.
 “There was a commotion of sorts in the group room. Here is where the details are very sketchy, and somehow Helen was pushed out of her chair. When she was jettisoned from her wheelchair, she fell forward while holding the needles, which pierced both of her eyes and plunged into her brain. She died face down on the floor of the group room.”
“Oh my god, that was terrible! Was there an investigation?”
“No, the death was ruled accidental, and the whole thing was tucked away quickly. With no family to push for anything more, it just faded away. But here is what I think you would call a plot twist. Helen’s death was obviously enough to make a spirit restless, if indeed they can be; however, there is more. 
“Helen was born around the turn of the twentieth century. She died in 1979. She and her sister were originally from Ohio. They were sent to live with an aunt here in Wisconsin. The aunt contracted TB and died when Helen was eighteen, and her sister was seventeen. Helen was engaged at the time and married that summer. Soon after the aunt passed away, her sister came to live with Helen and her husband. Helen wanted a better life for her sister, so she worked extra jobs and saved money to put her through nursing school. When she graduated, she married her high school sweetheart. It seemed all was well until her sister met Doctor Zuwiki.”
Patty's eyes widened as she realized what was coming next. Harvey could tell she had guessed the twist.
“That’s right, she was one of the married nurses involved with the good doctor who fell from the roof of the Lockwood!”
“Wow, are you kidding me? Both sisters died here in suspicious circumstances? That could be a spirit that has some unfinished business.”
“I guess if there ever was one that had the right to still be haunting a place, it would be Helen Cochran’s ghost.”
        “So how did the Lockwood finally close?”
The ownership group was not happy with the return on their investments. So, in 1998, the east wing was converted into a private mental hospital. The owners took that on in an attempt to make the cash flow work. Each mental patient came with a state allotment for care. The owners took the cash and provided the care. The numbers worked for a few years, but the state did not increase the amount, and after a couple of years, the facility was losing money on each mental patient, as their care was more expensive than what the facility was receiving. When the owners petitioned for an increase, the state came in and shut them down, citing abuse and neglect as the reason.”
“What happened to the residents at that time?”
“That was a true tragedy. The old folks were moved to other facilities around the state, and some of them moved to Minnesota. All but the most severe mental patients were given money for bus tickets and released.”
“They just sort of kicked them out?”
“Yep, for years after some of them were still wandering around here and Eau Claire, trying to survive. Several ended up homeless. I only know about one that moved in with his family down the road. I assume he ended up doing okay.”
“Harvey, you have a knack for telling some pretty good ghost stories. You should make it a side hustle.”
“I’m too old for a side hustle, but I hope I have sweetened the deal for your use.”
“That you have. Here is our offer.”
Harvey looked at the form and at Patty. 
“I just want to hear you say that you will take care of the place and keep it standing.”
“I promise as long as we own the Lockwood, it will be cared for.”
“That’s good enough for me. You now own the Lockwood. I guess the papers have to be completed, but as far as I’m concerned, we have a deal.”
Patty was very pleased.
“Thank you, sir. If I have a conference here for paranormal investigators, would you be willing to come and give a history of the place? There would be a small stipend for your time.”
“That could be fun.”
“Great, I will keep that in mind when we get the place set up for that. In the meantime, if you would consider writing a book about the place, and include the possible hauntings. I will market it on our website.”
“A book, huh. I had not thought of doing that. I’ll let you know.”
“Okay, well, I have some work to do. We are going to clean up the small kitchen and cafeteria on the east end and turn that into an apartment for the caretaker we intend to hire. I have a contractor in Eau Claire, and I tentatively scheduled to meet today to get the ball rolling. I also need to schedule our team to do our initial investigation. We will be filming it for our online channel.”
“Okay, it is exciting that you intend to move that quickly. I’ll have to watch the video when it’s posted. It was good dealing with you, thank you.”
“Likewise, I’ll be in touch when that conference gets put together.” 
Harvey left, andPatty took a few moments to walk around the Lockwood before driving into Eau Claire to meet with the contractor. She could envision the steady stream of visitors discovering the thrills of the paranormal at the Lockwood.


*****
The advertisement was for a position as a caretaker. It offered a competitive salary, room, food allowance, and two weeks' paid vacation. Barney Doyle wanted to get back to the area of his childhood home, even though his recollections of that childhood were not happy. Life’s road had been bumpy for Barney. He was a bit of an outcast throughout elementary and high school. He stayed fairly close to the area for college, where he was a loner. He felt lucky to meet a woman at the university whom he married right after graduation. 
They moved closer to her family and friends in Milwaukee. He took a job and worked hard, but he did not enjoy the work. The marriage was okay for many years until one day she looked at him and said she was done. There really wasn’t much discussion; she had decided she wanted more or at least different, and said she was leaving. He didn’t handle it well. It took some time for him to deal with all of the fallout of his wife’s decision. It was a dark, angry time. There were portions of his life during those days that he actually couldn’t remember. He hadn’t seen or heard from her since that time. 
Her family questioned him several times about where she had gone. He could not give them an answer. He had no idea; she said she was leaving, and she left. That was all he could tell them. Some went so far as to talk to the police, questioning if there was foul play. He spoke to a detective, but they found no evidence that he had done anything wrong. He felt increasingly alone after that. Each day melted into the next, no joy, no anticipation, just another day.   
By the time he read the ad, he had been alone for some time, working a job he did not like in a city he did not want to be in. When he read about the position for the caretaker job, he was ready for a change.
        Patty Robertson called Barney about a week after he sent his resume.
“Hello.”
“Hello, am I speaking with Barney Doyle?”
“Yes, this is Barney.”
“Hi Barney, this is Patty Robertson. You sent me a resume for the caretaker position.”
“Oh, yes. Hello, Patty.”
“I’m calling because I would be very interested in meeting you at the property to discuss the position.”
“Oh, well, I guess I could take some time off to come for an interview. When were you thinking?”
“I would like to do it as soon as possible. I don’t live near the property, but I am going to be there for two days next week. I was hoping you would be able to meet then. I need to get someone hired right away. We just bought the place, and I don’t want to leave it unsupervised for long. People could start vandalizing it or using it for all kinds of things we don’t want to deal with.”
“I see, okay, what days will you be there?”
“Next Tuesday and Wednesday. You are the first applicant I’ve called, and so you have your choice of time and day.”
“I can meet you on Tuesday. Shall we say 9:00 am?”
“That would be great.”
“Where should I be at that time?”
“Oh, that’s right, you don’t know where you could be working. It is the Lockwood Sanatorium. There is an apartment we have set up on the far east end of the east wing of the building. I currently have a makeshift office in there, but it will be the caretaker’s quarters.”
“The Lockwood, I remember that place. It was a huge complex. It was a good eldercare facility in its day. That place has been closed for several years. What kind of business are you running there now?”
“I would rather explain that to you when we meet if that’s alright.”
“Okay, is there anything I should bring for the interview?”
“Just yourself, well, your spouse is welcome as well.”
“Oh, I am not married. It’s just me.”
“That’s fine. I will see you on Tuesday at 9:00 am.
“See you then. Thank you.”
“Thank you.”
Barney was very interested in what anyone would do with a building that large.
It was a creepy place these days. Having been built in the early 1900s, it had that old institutional feel. He knew it had been several things over the years, all of them dealing with times of struggles for the people they served. 
Barney took a few days off and went back to the Eau Claire area. He had a hotel in the city and spent Sunday and Monday driving around all of his old haunts from his youth. The place did not look that much different. There were fewer family farms. The small unincorporated villages on the west side of Eau Claire County were virtually unchanged. While the city of Eau Claire had grown, the rural area around it had not. On Monday, he drove out to the Lockwood just to get a look at the place before he met with Patty Robertson on Tuesday. 
The building had not gotten any less creepy. Barney looked at it from the road since the gate was locked. He leaned on the rails and thought about his last fifteen years. He felt he was in a much better place now than he had been when the whole thing with his wife went down. That had been rough; he lost it for nearly a year. Drinking and painkillers had nearly killed him. He needed a lot of therapy to get himself back in control. Spending time in a quiet environment sounded pretty good. 


*****


        Patty Robertson was driving out to the Lockwood. She would be staying there while she was in the area. She had not exactly been honest with Barney. She knew she had made it sound as though she had more interviews, and she would be selecting from several interested parties. The truth was, Barney was the only applicant. If she didn’t feel good about him, she was not certain what she was going to do. The bookings were starting to come in, though very slowly, and she still needed to get someone in place. There had to be someone there to welcome people and maintain control of the property while the ghost hunts were in progress.
Barney was waiting at the gate when she arrived. As she stepped out to open the lock, she spoke to him.
“You must be Barney. It is nice to meet you. You certainly are on time. I like that.”
“I guess I was anxious to start the day. I stayed in Eau Claire this weekend, and after breakfast, I just drove out. It is nice and peaceful out here. I know the area very well. I grew up just a few miles from here.”
“Oh really, so this job would be sort of a coming home gig for you.”
“I guess you could say that. I am looking to get back into this area. I’ve been living and working in Milwaukee for the past twenty-five years. My wife was from there, and when we married, we moved there. I just got used to the job and the place, and when we broke up, I just stayed there. It took me a while to get to the point where I wanted to do something for myself.”
Patty felt maybe she had just received more information than she needed, but the guy seemed very sincere. Besides, she couldn’t get too picky unless she was going to stay out there until she found someone else.
“Well, shall we head up to the building?”
“Sounds good.”
The two of them drove up to the building and circled the horseshoe-shaped drive in front of the Lockwood.
Patty got out of her car and opened the trunk to fetch her suitcase. Barney noticed.
“Here, let me give you a hand with that.”
“Thank you. Be careful, I’m not a light packer.”
“Oh, it’s not too bad.”
The pair walked into the apartment at the end of the east wing.
“This will be the living quarters that are provided for the caretaker. The pay reflects the fact that the person in the position will not have to provide their own living arrangements. It is not quite finished. It should be fully done in about a week. It’s not bad to stay in now, just some paint and molding yet to be completed. I believe the salary is still generous, though the hours are twenty-four seven.”
“That is a little intimidating.”
“Oh. I don’t want to scare you off. What I mean is that the job is a caretaker, but there is an element of it that is also a security guard. When you have a place this large and secluded, unfortunately, some people are nosey or looking for a cheap thrill. The job will require being the security for the place now and then. While the work is not very demanding, the hours may be erratic. We will be needing an individual who is a self-starter and can maintain a schedule. I will also need someone who can act as our on-site contact for our clientele.”
“That would be a great place to start. Who is your clientele?”
“I’m sorry, I should have started there. We are the CSPG. That is the Central States Paranormal Group. We offer paranormal investigations.”
“You mean people come here to hunt ghosts?”
“We don’t like to say hunt ghosts, but that about sums it up.” 
“That’s interesting. I suppose a big old place like this would spark the imagination regarding something like that. I can’t say I believe in such things, but I have never given it much thought. Have you seen anything here?”
“Seen? No, but I have heard all kinds of sounds that I can’t easily explain.”
        “Huh, well, that would be a new one on me. So, what exactly would be my responsibilities with the ahh … what did you call them, paranormal investigators?”
        “You would be here to check them in and just keep an eye on them so they don’t do anything to destroy the property. Also, to make sure they don’t do silly, dangerous stuff. They will have signed waivers for most of that kind of thing, but it is always good to remind some people.”
“I understand that. I always say, you can’t fix stupid.”
        “That is a good way to put it. Of course, we want them to return, so it will be important to use some tact in dealing with them, if possible.”
        “Sure, how often do you have people doing their investigations?”
        “We hope to have one or two per week, maybe more in the summer. I have several plans for making the place a little easier to monitor. Cameras in the whole place will greatly improve our ability to keep people out of trouble or danger.”
        “That is a concern because I assume you are not expecting me, or the person you hire, to follow the people around. I can’t imagine that would be appreciated. I would be in their way.”
        “No, just follow to get them started and point out any areas to avoid for safety’s sake. Then check on them periodically. Most people who are into this have experience and know how to conduct themselves.”
        “We kind of got into the job description right away. Are there any questions you have for me?”
        “You mentioned you were looking to get back near where you grew up. How long did you live here?”
        “I was here through college, I guess. I didn’t come home much while I was attending college, but this was still my official residence. I didn’t have a close relationship with my father; we just didn’t see eye to eye. There was some drama earlier in my life. My mother died when I was fourteen. So, I managed to get along through high school and then left for college. I have my degree in business management. I have worked for a paper factory for the past twenty-five years. For the last fifteen years, I have lived alone in an apartment in Milwaukee. I’m just ready to do something else, and I’d like to do it around here. Your ad seemed very interesting since it included the living quarters.’
        “It sounds like you’ve been in a desk job for several years. How does working manual labor sound to you? A lot of your days will be spent cleaning out the debris in the Lockwood. As you can imagine, there are a lot of interior pieces that need to be removed, and the ceiling and some wall coverings are falling off and need to be disposed of.”
        “I have no fear of hard work. I would appreciate the opportunity to manage my time and get projects done on my own. For twenty-five years, I’ve had to get work done through others. I like the idea of only being responsible for myself.”
        “Well, I do have a guy who has done some work for me that you may be using part-time. He is a little slow-witted, but he works hard. He is an older gentleman who lives a few miles from here. I sort of stumbled onto him in town shortly after I bought the place. He had done some work for the previous owner, so I have references for him. He would just be here a day or two per week in the spring through fall.”
        “I’m sure that would work fine. I’ve dealt with all kinds.”
        “I guess the big question is, do you think you could handle being out here most of the time alone? This is a big place. As I said, it has its noises that make you wonder. Are you comfortable in being in that type of situation?”
        “I would look forward to it.”
        “In that case, the job is yours if you want it. You would start as soon as possible. I assume you will need to give notice at your current job.”
        “I actually already gave notice a week ago. I had decided to leave either way. I have enough money saved that I could go a long time without work. I just wanted to focus on getting to my next stage in life. So, I can start next week.”
        “That is fantastic. If you have time, I can give you a tour of the place and let you know what projects are a priority to get started on.”
        “That sounds good to me.”
        Patty took Barney around, and the two of them created a checklist of work that needed to be done.


*****


 Within a week of the purchase, the CSPG team was standing in the driveway of the Lockwood. The group consisted of Bob Madden, the lead investigator, and Kristie Madden, Bob’s wife, who had expertise in handling the paranormal equipment. Brad Fisher and Connie Waldran rounded out the team.
Barney Doyle was on the property when they arrived.
“You must be Bob Madden. Patty said you were coming today. I’m Barney, Barney Doyle, the new caretaker. I thought I would stop by and make sure you had everything you need.”
“Nice to meet you, Barney. Are you staying out here now?”
“Yes, part-time at this point. Once you are all set, I’ll be leaving you to do your work.”
“I appreciate the welcome. I think we are all set.”
“Okay, good luck on your investigation.”
“Thanks, we will probably see you again sometime.”
Barney shook his head in agreement and waved at the rest of the crew, and headed into the apartment on the east end of the facility.
“Bob, what time are you thinking we should start the investigation?”
“I think it will be dark in about two hours. That should give us time to get our cameras all in position and decide where we want to focus our EVP sessions. I want to do the Spirit Box session in the grand foyer to see if we can talk to Mary Lockwood. We also want a camera and recorder in the morgue.”
“I have all the equipment charged and extra batteries. We need to select a spot for our home base.”
“You are right, Kristie. Brad, can you grab those equipment cases? I’ll take these, and we should head in and pick a good spot to use as home base.”
The group each hauled a portion of the equipment into the building.
“It never gets old stepping into this foyer. I can feel the energy immediately.”
“I know what you mean, Connie. This space is amazing. Can you imagine what it was like in 1920? I always wonder if the spirits see these spaces as they are now or as they were when they were alive. I mean, if they are interacting with us, do they see us as we were back then, or do they see us as we do? I suppose I will never get an answer to that.”
“Let’s go upstairs. There is an office just before the ‘Grand Central’ space that could work for a home base.”
Bob was leading the group to the office he was referring to when they all heard a loud slam.
“Did you hear that?”
“I did, Connie. It sounded like it came from straight ahead down the center hall.”
“I thought it was to the right, and echoed up in this direction.”
“It was hard to tell, Bob. It’s interesting that we just got in here and noises are starting. I hope that means it will be a good night to capture things. We want the video we post to show some good stuff.”
“It would be great to get something that helps us go viral. The audience is still pretty small.”
“As long as we stick to only posting with integrity, we will grow the brand. I don’t want any over hype in what we produce. I hate those ghost-hunting shows and videos that sensationalize their content. You know the ones that have demons at every location or where they are getting ghosts attached to them all the time. We are dedicated to REAL experiences.”
“I agree, Bob. But if we do find something, I think it is okay to crow loud and long about it. We are, after all, trying to make these locations pay for themselves.”
“I am fine with promoting real finds.”
“I just want to see some great evidence just for me. So far, I have seen lights go off and heard the dead bell chime, but I want to hear something with my own ears and see something with my own eyes.”
As if on cue, as the team entered the office to unload the equipment, a loud scream came from the grand foyer area. The group all ran toward the sound. They all stood along the rail of the balcony that overlooked the grand foyer. There was nothing there.
“We all heard that, right?”
“I sure did, Brad.”
“So did I. Kristie, did we have a recorder going?”
“Just my handheld, Bob.”
“Let’s check to see if you caught that.”
Kristie backed up the recording and hit play.
The group could hear their conversation, and right as Brad says the word “eyes”, there is a faint scream on the recording.
“That’s it, we got it! It sounded much louder in person, but we got it.”
“That is fantastic. I think it is a residual, don’t you?”
“I would have to say it is. According to Patty’s information from the previous owner, Mary Lockwood screamed when she hung herself in the grand foyer. That would fit with what we just heard.”
“I agree, Kristie, that was a great score. Should we get the equipment set up in the office and then select all of the locations we are going to place the rempods, the dead bell, and the cat balls?”
It took about an hour for the cameras to be placed and the monitors set up for the night’s investigation. Bob then started to get everyone organized.
“Okay, I am heading down to start in the morgue. Kristie, you’re going to stay here and watch the video monitors and keep the log of activity with time and location, the way you normally do.”
“I have a new wrinkle I’m going to be experimenting with tonight. I plan to keep a verbal record. I think I will have a more detailed account that way.”
“That sounds good. Connie, you and Brad start here on the second floor. Later, we will try the Estes method in the East Wing. That was the area that was converted to the Mental Ward. We will meet there in about an hour. Does that work for everyone?”
Kristie responded first.
“I’m good. Before we split up, everyone, check your walkies.”
Each of the members tried their walkie-talkies.
“Mine is all good, how about yours, Connie?”
“Seems good, are you ready to start on the second floor, Brad?”
“Let’s do it.”
Connie and Brad walked down the west wing, second-floor hall. They planned to select a room about halfway down and attempt to communicate with a spirit or group of spirits. 
“Is your walkie working properly, Bob?”
“Yes, it is time for me to head to the morgue.”
“I can’t believe you want to go down there alone.”
“I think we get better results when we are alone in the dark.”
“I’m not so sure I want to tempt fate in that way.”
“We have never been harmed by a spirit.”
“Those scratches on your back at the Scythe Murder House in Arkansas weren’t exactly a pat on the back.”
“That was different. We were trying to taunt the spirits that night. You remember the experiment we tried.”
“Yes, I do, that was not one of your better ideas either.”
“If we are not going to push the boundaries now and then, we aren’t going to learn what is really out there.”
“I know, I just don’t like you putting yourself at risk.”
‘I’ll be fine. You just keep your eyes on the screens. I hope we can leave here with some great stuff for our video. It would be fantastic if we could get two videos out of our content from tonight.”
“We’ll see, the scream was a good start.”
Bob gave Kristie a thumbs up and started down the steps to the main floor and then down to the tunnels. For the next five hours, the team attempted to communicate with spirits using every form of equipment they had at their disposal.
“So, you haven’t seen anything on the screens that we can use?”
“I’m telling you this place has been stone quiet. There’s been nothing on any of the cameras. Have any of the three of you seen or heard anything?”
“Connie and I haven’t had a rempod go off or a catball blink. The EVP recordings have not caught anything. This place has been silent.”
“What do you think, Bob?”
“I’m shocked, actually. After hearing the scream as soon as we got in here, I assumed we were in for a ton of activity. Now here we are. I can’t even say there is enough for a short video. You all know this can happen. We’ve been on a quiet one before. Sometimes the spirits just don’t want to interact with us.”
“What should we do now?”
“I think it is time to pack up. No point in wasting more time.”
The team moved swiftly to collect the gear they had installed around the building. The packing was done in short order. Just as they were about to start hauling the cases down the steps, the atmosphere of the whole building changed. 
“Wait! Did you hear that?”
“What are you talking about, Connie?”
“That running, someone was running down the center hall downstairs. I know there was.”
“I heard it too. I was trying to figure out what I heard, but that was it.”
“Kristie, are you sure hearing Connie talk about it didn’t sway your opinion?”
“No, I heard running for sure.”
“Okay, we need to check it out. Brad, you come with me. Ladies, you stay up here and keep an eye on the halls. We don’t want to miss anything at this point. Grab the handheld cameras.”
Bob and Brad moved quickly down the steps to the first floor. Just as they hit the bottom step, they heard the two women screaming. They turned to run back up the steps to find the ladies pointing down the west wing.
“What are you pointing at?”
“There, don’t you see it?”
“Calm down, Kristie. What are you talking about?”
“It’s gone now. There was a shadow. It ran past me. We both saw it, right, Connie?”
“Yes, it was large and darker than the dark hall; it moved without walking. I mean, I didn’t see the legs moving. I’m not sure it was touching the floor. I have never seen anything like it. When it went past, I felt cold; it was really cold.”
“Was it human-shaped?”
“I think so.”
“Yeah, yeah, except the no legs.”
“Which way did it go?”
“It went down that hall; it may have gone into a room at the end of the hall.”
The group moved down the hall, checking each room as they made their way. They found nothing in any of the rooms. After another hour and a half, they saw nothing and heard nothing.
“Okay, now we are back to where we were before. We didn’t capture the shadow or the footsteps. Is it possible there is a human in this building trying to scare us out of the building?”
“I don’t think that shadow was human, at least not a live human.”
“Well, we haven’t seen or heard anything since you thought you saw something.”
“We didn’t think we saw something, we saw it!”
“Okay, well, we haven’t seen anything since.”
“I don’t care, we saw …”
“Wait! Listen!”
As they were discussing what the women saw or thought they saw, there was a whispering coming from somewhere down the East Wing.
“I can’t make out what they are saying.”
“I heard ‘get out’.”
“I think that was what I heard as well.”
“Did anyone catch the whispers on camera or a recorder?”
“I was too busy trying to hear it.”
“So was I.”
“Just a second, I’m trying to see if I caught it on camera.”
Brad was rewinding and checking the camera.
“Well, did you catch anything?”
“I … I think I ,,, no it isn’t on here. There is a section that is just snow, and then it started recording again.”
“I don’t know what is going on, Bob, but it's like something is playing with us. We aren’t capturing any evidence even though it's going on all around us.”
At that moment, a door at the other end of the center hall slammed shut.
“That’s it. I’m out of here. Whatever is messing with us has us running in circles.”
“Hang on, Connie. We can’t just run away from these things. We are here to capture the evidence.”
“Bob, they are not going to let us. Don’t you get it? They are just playing with us. I say we get out of here.”
“What do you two think? Kristie, what do you want to do?”
“I … ahhh … “
        Before she can answer, three doors in the East Wing slam one right after the other. Bob took off running with the camera in the direction of the doors, which were slamming. When he got there, he tried filming only to find out the battery in his camera had been drained.
“Damn it! I don’t have any battery.”
“I just changed the batteries. They were all fresh, and we have hardly used them.”
“Well, it’s dead now, Kristie.”
“They must be draining them, taking the energy to do what they are doing.”
“Okay, you win. We are leaving for the night, but we will be back.”
The group grabbed the equipment cases and went down the steps. There were the sounds of footsteps and doors swinging open and shut as they left. It almost seemed as though they were being taunted. They loaded the van and climbed in. As they started the vehicle and drove down the long lane, a dark figure stepped out of the building and watched them from the shadows. Once they were out of sight, the figure slowly walked down the front steps, continued around the building, and disappeared into the woods.
Though Bob had sworn he would be back, they did not return. The video they posted online featured the four of them talking about their experience and sharing the single faint scream they managed to record. The lack of visible evidence did not increase interest in ghost hunting at the Lockwood.
Patty had to do a significant amount of marketing and offer discounts to keep enough traffic moving through the Lockwood. It was frustrating because it was obvious the place was filled with paranormal activity, but it only showed itself when it chose to. The spirits were stubborn and highly intelligent. The only dependable thing was the scream of Mary Lockwood. It was as though she was still trying to keep the place relevant. 


*****
        
For the next two years, Patty developed a strong trust in Barney. Unfortunately, the bookings were not nearly as strong as she would have liked, and the financial needs required. 
She had been lucky enough to find a caretaker who didn’t mind pulling double duty as a security guard for the place. Due to the booking struggles, there really wasn’t much money to use for anything beyond paying for the one employee. She had placed some of the upgrades on hold, such as security cameras and expanding the functional electricity beyond the living quarters of the caretaker. That meant the facility was very dark during the investigations. That may add to the atmosphere, but it was not as safe as she would have liked. If something didn’t spark interest in the place soon, they were going to have to reconsider their long-term plans for the place. 
For Barney, life went on at the Lookwood. He was alone nearly all of the time. He didn’t mind the solitude. There was something about the idea that he was actually alone and not just lonely in a crowd that was better than his life before. The fact that he didn’t have to see others enjoying the company of friends made his lack of friendship easier to handle. He had found one person with whom he could share conversations and time. Dober Grumby was a local recluse who stopped by now and then. Over time, he became a sort of part-time helper. 
________________


Chapter 2


        She was confused. Nothing was as it had been. It seemed as though just a few seconds earlier, she had been happily scurrying through the woods in north central Wisconsin. Leaves were falling all around her, and she could sniff all of the familiar smells in her woods. Now she had a searing pain across her face. She couldn’t see. The muscles around her eyes stung and flexed without actual movement. She was stumbling through what smelled like her woods, but it now felt dark, scary, and unfamiliar. She was vulnerable. 
Without sight, she had to depend on her other senses to find her way. She needed shelter somewhere to hide until she could figure out what to do. She couldn’t just stumble around the woods aimlessly. She caught the smell of smoke. It wasn’t just smoke, though; it carried another scent. It was food; she had smelled it before. On instinct, she followed the aroma. She bumped and tripped over roots and downed branches. It was a difficult path she was walking through the bramble of the forest. 
Suddenly, she realized the trees had given way to a grassy section. It had tall weeds, but it no longer had bushes or trees. Without sight, she had no idea how exposed she might be. The food smells kept her moving until she ran into something large and hard. She felt it all over and could not find an edge. It had to be a wall of some kind. She began walking along the wall with one shoulder sliding along its surface.
It took a while, but she reached a hole in the structure. She leaned into the hole, and it seemed to allow her access. Had she been able to see, she would have realized she was entering the defunct Lockwood Sanatorium. It is hard to tell if seeing the immense size of the place would have intimidated her. As it was, she could tell she had entered a building. The smells changed. It had a more damp and musty smell. She stumbled around until she found a wall. Since her method had worked outside, she leaned into the wall and started walking.
Eventually, she was getting tired. Between the stress of her state of vulnerability, the pain she was experiencing, and the exertion of her trek, she needed to rest. That was going to be a risky proposition. Not having any idea how exposed she was made selecting a place to settle in for a while a true gamble. She was also hungry. She had not found the origin of the food smells, and now the dankness of the building covered any faint wafts of the food that may still be outside. She would have to settle for just getting some rest. She was still walking, shoulder against the wall, hoping to find a location that felt enclosed and dry. 
Once again, a doorway formed a void in the wall she had been leaning into. She entered, assuming there could be a spot for her to curl up and sleep. She seemed to have walked onto a large piece of fabric. It didn’t smell as bad as the floor and wall, and it was dry. She burrowed into it for warmth and comfort. The pain was constant, and her stomach ached for sustenance, but exhaustion won out, and she fell asleep.   


***** 


        As if it were an ancient temple hidden in the rainforest of South America, the structure rose from the trees and bushes surrounding and encroaching on its walls. The grey limestone blocks that made up the central building gave way to grey bricks covering the three wings, which extended from the central core. The complex lay dormant, a dark remnant of its former existence. Staging a futile battle to hold back its ultimate decomposition, a lone figure was clearing out rubble. 
Barney Doyle had been working as the maintenance man slash security guard at Lockwood for the past two years. He had gotten to know every inch of the old sanatorium. He remembered back when the place was considered a decent spot for older folks to spend their last few years. That time passed, and it went downhill until the State closed it due to unsafe conditions and, what they called, abuse. 
Over the course of time, the woods surrounding the place seemed to creep ever closer. It was slowly beginning to engulf the buildings. Barney thought it was a shame to watch the nearly two hundred thousand square feet of property slowly die and rot into oblivion. His job was not so much to repair anything, but rather to dispose of things as they fell from the ceilings and walls. Generally, he was to keep the place safe to walk through. It no longer served any of its original purposes. No healthcare or nursing care was performed in the place, at least by living, breathing humans. There could be reflections of those activities still being played out in the dead of night, by the residual energy of the long-passed occupants.  
It was these reflections that provided the income for the owners of the place. The final metamorphosis for the hulking building involved nighttime tourists hoping to catch evidence of ghosts. Barney considered himself open-minded about the possibility of paranormal activity. He had seen things and heard things in the building. At times, the place felt dead and vacant, while at other times, it was electric and energy filled the air. He wasn’t willing to say that it was actually caused by spirits or “ghosts”.
Barney always chalked it up to more natural causes. The building settling, the wind hitting the pipes, and air leaks in the building just right, or electrostatic energy in the air from storm clouds. He hadn’t seen or heard anything that he couldn’t explain as natural rather than supernatural.
Most days, Barney had an assistant who worked with him part-time. Dober was an interesting sort. Most around the Mount Hope Corners area viewed him as a halfwit. That was just how he wanted it. Dober was one of the most intelligent people Barney knew. The man had knowledge of nearly any topic. Dober was constantly reading. He was like a walking encyclopedia. To Barney, the man had become a sort of surrogate father figure. 
At about seventy-five, Dober had witnessed or been involved in nearly every phase of the Lockwood’s existence. He could walk the property in his bib overalls and his Redwing Classic workboots and tell stories of events from the early 1930s to the present. He could remember names and dates as if they were in some great database he could access telepathically. Dober would bring a craggy old hand to his week-long growth of whiskers on his chin and scratch as the memories flowed from his sharp mind. 
The old man would do that often as he and Barney worked around the Lockwood. He rarely exhibited his talents in front of other people, choosing instead to play the town idiot. Barney had asked about his acting the fool.
“Dober, why do you let folks think you’re touched in the head? You always act like you don’t know which way is up around other people. We both know you could talk rings around everyone, including most of the professors over in Eau Claire.”
“What would I gain from people knowing I’m not the fool I pretend to be? They’d start expecting me to help with the church or the school. They might want to know more about me. Some could start asking for my opinions. That all sounds like a bother to me. I like being private and keeping my opinions to myself. I expect nothing from them. Presenting myself as dumb Dober keeps them from expecting anything from me.”
When he finished his answer, he pulled on the old baseball cap that had a heavy sweat stain in the front at the base of the crown and loose strings hanging from the bill where it was nearly worn through, and went about his work.
Barney respected the simple existence Dober had chosen. He lived a life not too different from himself. Since his wife left him fifteen years earlier, he had lived alone. When the job at the Lockwood came up, it seemed the perfect fit. He could sell his house, which felt so empty, and move into the section of the Lockwood that the owners had redone. It was sparse and plain, but it was heated and air-conditioned and had a small kitchen that met the needs of a single guy. The work was at his own pace and steady. His quarters were comfortable and included as part of his compensation. All-in-all, it was a sweet arrangement.
He enjoyed his life at the Lockwood, though he questioned if he was exhibiting hermit tendencies. It seemed his interest in getting into the small villages around the Corners or to the city of Eau Claire diminished with each passing day. He liked the solitude, sprinkled with occasional conversation when Dober came to work with him.
It was in October, late in the day, when the sun was already low enough that the sky displayed a pinkish purple color that radiated out and played hide and seek with the clouds. That time of year, when the afternoon air took on a crisp chill that hinted of the winter cold just around the corner, was Barney’s favorite time.
He and Dober were working on cleaning up the drop ceiling panels that had fallen in the middle section of the Lockwood. The building had been constructed in 1918 as a tuberculosis sanatorium. Back then, it was about one-third of its current size. When streptomycin was discovered as a treatment for the disease, it was converted into a hospital for the mentally insane. The second portion of the building was added for that reincarnation, doubling the size of the footprint in 1957. By the 1970s, there was a need in the area for an elder care facility. That was when the final section was added to the complex. It ran as a state mental institution, hospice, and nursing home until its closure in 2001.
Barney and Dober were working in the nursing home portion of the building, clearing the debris from the hallway.
“Dober, we are running out of light. The sun is setting earlier every day.”
“That is true. Here in Wisconsin, we lose 2.8 to 2.9 minutes of daylight each day.”
“Are you sure you can’t get a little more specific on that answer?”                                                                   
“I probably could, because it depends on what day of the month we are talking about. In general, the average should suffice.”
“You amaze me.”
“Oh, I’ve worked with you for, what is it? About a year and a half now. I think you’re easily amazed.” 
 “You got me there. I keep my knowledge down to just what I need to get by. So anything beyond that is amazing to me.”
As the men worked, they heard the usual noises the old building normally made. Those noises happened all of the time, and the two men had grown accustomed to ignoring them. Sometimes they would hear what sounded like a woman crying or footsteps above them, but they assumed the wind or falling debris was the culprit. Neither of them was willing to accept the idea of intelligent hauntings.
Barney’s open-mindedness only went so far as to entertain the idea that human activity could be so traumatic that it is recorded in the structure. He had read a scientific account once about ancient native drums being recorded in the pottery produced at the time. He had never followed up on whether it was proven or not, but why couldn’t an event be of such monumental impact on an individual that it leaves an impression of the activity where it happened? In that case, a residual recording would play in some time interval, over and over again. 
In Barney’s mind, that would not be construed as a haunting. If he were honest, he really didn’t want there to be ghosts. He had spent so much time in the building and did not want that to be a concern. He was happy to live in a world where the dead remained dead. He wanted nothing to do with an intelligent ghost. 
“Dober, we’ve worked together for close to two years. I’ve never asked you about your thoughts on the concept of the paranormal. Do you believe in ghosts?”
“Believe is an interesting word. It refers to the acceptance of something as truth. Now we have a problem. Truth can be a relative thing. Often, reality can be perceived differently by each individual. When you ask me if I believe in ghosts, you are essentially asking me if I think ghosts are a universal truth. If I say no, then I am condemning all who accept the concept as truth. Who am I to do that?”
        “Wow, Dober, that was a long answer, if it was an answer. I essentially asked you what time it was, and you told me how to build a clock.”
“If you wanted a short answer, you should have asked someone who is a smaller thinker.”
“Once again, you have me with that one.”
The two men continued their work as they cleared the hall of the ceiling panels. The work was dirty, and the air was filled with nasty dust. Barney had his nose and mouth covered with a kerchief. Dober had a full respirator and looked a little like he was heading to a forest fire. 
The men started to shovel and push the debris down the hallway to the window at the end. Dober had hauled a dumpster around with the small front loader. They intended to shovel the waste out the window into the waiting dumpster. The plan had been a good one. They were about halfway down the hall when Barney heard a scream.
“Wait, Dober, did you hear that?”
“Hear what?”
“It sounded like a scream, a woman’s scream.”
“I didn’t hear anything. Now, who seems to be believing in ghosts?”
“I didn’t say it was a ghost. I heard a woman scream.”
Just as Barney started to look into the former patient rooms on either side of the hallway, he heard a hissing sound. It was the kind that the ghost hunters who came around associated with what they called demons. Barney was feeling pretty edgy about what was happening. Then, out of nowhere, she moved and let out a loud trill sound coming right at Barney from one of the rooms. Barney jumped back and let out a yelp. Dober turned and watched as a female raccoon scurried out of the patient room behind the running Barney. 
The raccoon swerved back and forth, trying to find a path it could follow. Once its shoulder hit the wall, it moved quickly down the hall toward the window that the two men had been pushing the trash toward. 
Barney regained his composure and started to follow the creature, who was struggling to get away. 
“Dober, look at its eyes. It doesn’t have any eyes.”
“You’re right, it looks like there are cuts around them. I think someone did that to the poor animal. It must be terribly afraid.”
“What kind of person would do that to an animal? That they not only mutilated its eyes, but then turned it loose to fend for itself blindly in the woods is awful. Someone like that has to be sick in the head.”
“You are correct. Someone like that does not deserve to be treated like a person. They should be put down, like the beast they are.”
“That is well, pretty harsh, but I understand the sentiment. What should we do? We can’t just let the poor creature stumble around until it gets killed and eaten by another predator.”
“That is a great question. If we try to catch it, we will scare it all the more. I think we are best off getting some food and water and leaving it be right here. We could finish clearing the hallway in the morning when she will probably be gone.”
“I suppose you’re right. It feels like we will be abandoning her, though.”
“Let’s just get the food and water and watch how she reacts.”
Barney grabbed some food from his lunch bag and pulled out a water bottle. He went to the end of the hall they had been cleaning, and set up a make-shift feeding station. He walked back past the raccoon. She hissed as she smelled him getting closer. Barney returned to where Dober was standing. The raccoon was between them and the window, and they backed off and gave her some space. She stood panting for a few seconds, and then her sense of smell took in the aroma of an egg salad sandwich. Slowly, she moved her shoulder to the wall toward the hope of a meal. 
Standing over the partial sandwich, she felt around until it was in her grasp. She sniffed it and then dunked it in the water, and then started to eat.
“I always get a kick out of the fact that raccoons wash their food. It seems odd that they are that clean.”
        “They don’t wash it to clean it; they wash it to soften the pads of their front feet. It enhances their sense of touch. Their paws are filled with sensitive nerves; the water activates their ability to ‘see’ the food with their sense of touch.”
“Really? All of these years, I thought they were just fussy about clean food.”
“Well, I’m sure you’re not the first or the last to think that.”
“I'd better call Sally about this.”
“What is the deputy going to do about a raccoon?”
“Somebody did this to that creature. It isn’t right to just let it go.”
“You go and call her, and we will have an investigation going on around here. I don’t like people crawling around me.”
“I can’t say I like it much either, but somebody is not right in the head if they think it’s funny to gouge the eyes out of a raccoon. I mean, they had to have knocked it out to do that. That takes planning. It wasn’t just a cruel whim. It was a premeditated act of harm.”
“I just don’t think it is something the sheriff’s department is going to want to handle anyway.”
“So you don’t think this is a big deal?”
“I’m not saying that, I just don’t think it rises to the level of calling the police.”
“I don’t know. Maybe I’ll feed her for a couple of days and think about it.”
“There you go. That sounds like a good plan.”
The two men abandoned the cleanup in the hallway for the rest of that day. Barney went on to work in the tunnels below the Lockwood. There was always stuff to knock off the walls and ceiling down there that would possibly hurt the ghost-hunting traffic. That usually happened on weekends. There was an occasional special rental by a group in the middle of the week. The fall was the busiest. Halloween brought out a lot of thrill seekers. The temperature in the building was usually neither too hot nor too cold, as it was at other times of the year.
The days rolled on, and the raccoon hung around where Barney had set up a feeding station and a sort of hutch for her to nest in. Dober hadn’t been around for the past three days. He only worked Monday through Wednesday at the Lockwood. He was retired and spent most of his time at his cabin, about three miles from the sanatorium. He was known to do a little hunting, in season or not. He generally was pretty sly about it and always used the meat of any animals he trapped or occasionally shot. The Game Warden had tried to catch him several times, but he could always outsmart the warden.


*****


Barney came to expect seeing the raccoon daily. It wasn’t a pet, and he never attempted to make it one. It was a wild animal, and he respected that. He was just providing the creature with the assistance it needed, having been damaged at the hand of another human. The days turned into weeks, and then the snow came. Winter in the north-central part of the state can be brutal. Barney attempted to give the raccoon more sheets to use to make its area warmer. Unfortunately, the blindness was one too many hurdles for survival, and in the middle of January, Barney came upon a sad sight when he went out to drop off food for the raccoon. There, frozen in the snow, was the blinded animal. It had missed its hutch by about eight feet and curled up near the foundation of the building. Barney took the small corpse and placed it in the woods. The natural order of things would dispose of the remains. He didn’t bother with any type of marker or remembrance. It was a wild animal that he helped for a period of time, and nature had determined the outcome. That was just how it went. 
Patty Robertson had several tasks on her plate. One of them was to call Barney Doyle at the Lockwood. Patty managed several properties for the Central States Paranormal Group (CSPG). Barney reported to her about the facility and handled the smaller groups that booked investigations. She dialed his number to discuss the winter season.
“Hello, this is Barney Doyle.”
“Hi, Barney. This is Patty. I wanted to touch base with you. The Lockwood doesn’t have many guests this winter season. Until we get it closed from the elements all around the building, the weather makes it quite challenging to investigate without ending up with frostbite. So, for this year, your main task will be to just watch over the place. Until we get the cameras installed, I’ll need you to do a couple of walk-throughs a day just to make sure animals didn’t take over a section, or kids didn’t try to make a portion of it a party room or something stupid like that.”
“Okay, on the decent weather days, I’ll continue with the clean out. As far as the walk-throughs, I have been doing that. On occasion, I have to chase people off the property. Some local kids have been pretty persistent about hanging around here. The biggest problem is cleaning up after them. They are drawing stuff on the walls and the floor. Since the building is so secluded, sitting here on over one hundred acres, it is a lot to watch over. Thankfully, there is rarely anything malicious; it is mostly just folks looking for a thrill.”
“Come springtime, we anticipate making a bigger push for investigations.” 
“That would be good. I do look forward to the spring. That is when Dober will come back to work three days a week. I like solitude, but I also like a conversation now and then.”
“Have you had any experiences that I could tell people about? As sort of a marketing kind of thing. I only want to hear about real events.”
 “I wouldn’t make up ghost stories. When I am here for days alone, if I walk through the sanatorium, there are times when I swear someone or something speaks to me. I generally chalk it up to being tired. I try to talk myself out of it being creepy or scary, except when I hear my name. For some reason, the personal nature of hearing one’s name changes the feel of it. I sort of freeze when that happens and look around, trying to determine from which direction it came. Too often, it is soft and has a kind of echo to it. That makes figuring out an origin impossible. Then there is the shadow. I’ve chased the shadow more than once. I swear it is someone sneaking around, but every time I think I have them cornered, no one is there.” 
“Those are good stories. I think I can use them.”
“Oh, well, glad I could help.”
Barney thought about what he had just shared with Patty. After a while, loneliness can do things to a person. He realized he had turned inward too much. The voices were probably an example of the problem. He needed to spend some time with people. Probably more people than Dober. He actually knew many folks in the area. He had grown up very close by and gone to school with several of the prominent citizens in the small towns around the Lockwood. Sally was probably the one person he enjoyed talking with the most. His thoughts were pulled back to the phone call when Patty spoke.  
“Okay, well, if you need anything, don’t hesitate to call. If I get a group willing to brave the cold, I’ll let you know.”
“Sounds good, goodbye.”
“Goodbye.”
Barney got off the phone and considered whether to call Sally. He hadn’t spoken to her for nearly a month. He didn’t want her to think he was ignoring her friendship. He looked at his phone to dial when he heard a vehicle pull into the Lockwood gate. It was a Sheriff’s deputy SUV. Barney walked out to the drive in front of the sanatorium to see what it was about.
The vehicle pulled up, and Sally Thomas stepped out.
“Sally? What are you doing here?”
“Is that any way to greet an old high school buddy?”
“I am surprised, that’s all. To be perfectly honest, I was just about to call you when you pulled in.”
“You were, what about?”
Barney wasn’t really sure how to answer. He thought about their relationship. They had a friendship that ran very deep, having known each other since grade school. There was a time when he had thought they could be more than friends, but life happens. He had gone off to college and met the woman who became his wife. That had set the course for him and Sally to remain just friends. Now she stood before him, asking why he was going to call her. 
“I just needed a friend to talk to. It seems I have gotten myself into a rut of being alone. Before I went full-on hermit, I thought I should reach out to the real world. And my favorite part of the world is you.”
“I suppose I should take that as a compliment.”
“I meant it as just truth, but it does mean you are important to me.”
He stopped; perhaps he had said too much. Being alone makes a person a little over-anxious to tell people what they’re thinking. He decided to change topics.
“So, I explained why I was calling. Why did you drive all the way out here?”
She smiled at her friend. They had history, and whenever a person has that kind of connection, it can sometimes get complicated.   
She had never married. She joined the police force in Eau Claire right out of college and worked there for nearly twenty-five years. When the sheriff’s department was looking for deputies, she applied and moved back home, hoping to reconnect with him. That was about the time Barney started working at the Lockwood. She wanted to blurt out her feelings for him, but lost the nerve. 
“I had a call out this direction, and since I was here, I had to stop by. It has been so long since we talked. I can’t believe I’m saying this, but I missed you.”
Barney laughed as she spoke. 
“Aren’t we a pair? Standing out here, grilling each other over why we would want to talk.”
They both laughed.
“Come inside to talk; we don’t need to stand out here where it is so chilly.”
“So what do you do out here all day?”
“I am responsible for making sure nobody is out here that shouldn’t be, and maintaining the place as best as it can be. I clean out the halls as debris accumulates, so the investigations can happen without people tripping on stuff. So, I am a part-time security guard and part-time janitor, I guess.”
“Do you like being out here alone so much? I mean, the place is pretty creepy.”
“It’s not so bad. I try not to wander the place in the dark too much. Your mind can play tricks on you out here. I’ve heard things and seen shadows. That bothers me sometimes. I guess mainly because I don’t have anyone to talk it over with after. You know, someone to help me calm down and rationalize the whole thing. I figure most of that stuff is just in my head. It’s easy to get carried away out here because the place looks like the setting of every horror movie ever.”
“You got that right. If I heard voices, I would be out of here in a heartbeat. I worry about you here. Don’t you think you would like a job that lets you have a little more free time? I mean a job where you could be in town a little more often. So you could see … ahh … friends?”
“Don’t worry about me. I think I can handle this place. I do get lonely now and then, but I do have free time. I read and walk quite a bit. That keeps me informed and in shape.” 
He looked at Sally. Her hair now had a hint of grey in its brunette waves that nearly reached her collar. Her pretty face still had the faint freckles that she had hated in middle school. Her eyes were still as bright, clear, and blue as they were when they met. 
It was during those lonely times that he would think of Sally. It just always seemed like the two of them could talk. It didn’t seem to matter what the topic was; he just enjoyed her company. He didn’t know how to tell her that.
“When the lonely feelings happen, I try to keep myself even busier.”
“So there isn’t much excitement out here then.”
“Excitement, well, there are the high school kids that think they are into the occult, I have to run off about once a week. I did have the raccoon incident.”
“I probably need to know more about the kids, but first, what happened with a raccoon?”
Sally was being sarcastic. She assumed a little story about a cute raccoon was about to be spun.
“Dober and I were cleaning out the west wing of the first floor. The ceiling tiles in this place are always falling, so they need to be cleared out. Anyway, we were working when I heard a woman scream.”
“Wait, a woman was screaming?”
“I heard one, but no one was there.”
“Barney, you are saying that like it is no big deal. Are you sure no one was there?”
“Yes, I hear those kinds of sounds all the time. I assume it just sounds like a woman. It more than likely is just the wind being distorted by pipes or something. That really doesn’t have much to do with the raccoon, though.”
“Okay, I just don’t like the idea of a woman screaming without knowing exactly why, but go ahead. Tell me about the raccoon. We can come back to the woman issue.”
“So I was looking into the patient rooms on either side of the hall to see if there was anyone, and then I heard a hissing sound. That kind of bothered me. You see the investigators that come out here talk about what they find, and hissing is supposed to be a demon. I don’t buy into that stuff, but when you hear hissing, it’s difficult not to think about it.”
“So, is this raccoon a demon?”
“No, of course not. I was just saying it had me a little spooked, and then out of nowhere, a raccoon came at me. I hate to admit it, but I ran like a fool.”
Sally started giggling at Barney’s story about being scared by a raccoon.
“All right, all right, that is enough laughing at me. You would have run away as well, face it.”
“Okay, probably, but thinking of you running away from a raccoon is still pretty funny.”
“Here is where it takes a really dark turn.”
“Are we going to be talking about ghosts?”
“No, you really are impatient when it comes to stories. Is that your detective instinct coming out?”
“Sorry, just tell your story.”
“The raccoon acted really strangely. It ran down the hall, swerving left to right like it didn’t know where it was going. Once it got next to the wall, it moved along the hall, leaning its front shoulder on the wall. When we looked a little closer, it didn’t have any eyes.”
“Like it was born without them?”
“No, this is the dark part. They had been gouged out.”
“What do you mean, like another animal had done it in a fight?”
“No, like a human cut them out, or maybe burned them out.”
“Oh my god. Why didn’t you call me? That is a horrible thing to do to an animal.”
“I thought I should, but Dober talked me out of it.”
“Dober? You listen to him?”
“Don’t let Dober’s act fool you. He is probably the smartest person I know. If there is something he doesn’t know anything about, I haven’t found it. He thought it was terrible that the animal had been mutilated, but that it didn’t rise to the level of crime  that would require the sheriff to be involved.”
“If he is as smart as you say, it concerns me that he said that. This is considered a precursor act. It could be a sign that someone may move on to larger animals or even people. It is definitely something we would want to know about. What happened to the raccoon?”
“I fed and watered her for several days. Unfortunately, she froze to death because she couldn’t find her nest. I threw the carcass into the woods to decompose.”
“I don’t suppose you took any photographs.”
“No, I hadn’t thought to start an album of her.”
“Don’t joke, the cuts around the eyes could be matched up with the weapon or edged tool that did the act.”
“Oh, I guess I hadn’t thought about that. There is no chance the carcass is still where I threw it. Another animal probably made off with it that day.”
“How much do you really know about Dober?”
“Dober? I’ve worked with him for a couple of years. He is what I would call a recluse. He fakes the halfwit role to keep people at arm's length. Why do you ask?”
“The fact that he didn’t want you to call law enforcement makes me wonder if he played a part in what happened to the raccoon.”
“Dober? No, he was just as upset about the poor animal as I was. In fact, he was ready to see whoever did to be put down like a rabid dog.”
“He said they should be put down?”
“Yes, I think that was exactly what he said.”
“That didn’t bother you?”
“I said it was a little over the top, but I understood the sentiment. I mean, who does something like that to a poor animal and then turns them loose in the woods? That was like condemning the raccoon to a horrible death. Which it ended up suffering, as it turns out.”
“Where does he live?”
“Just three miles down the road. You aren’t going to go bother him, are you?”
“I think I need to question him. It sure seems odd that he did not want to call the law. I think he meant it when he said whoever did it should be put down. I think he was talking about himself. People who commit acts that far out of the norm sometimes want to be caught and punished. He could be asking for help.”
“I think you are off target with him. He is more stable than that, in my opinion. If you are going to talk to him, I guess I can’t stop you.” 
“I have to do my job. You know I can’t just let this go.”
“I guess I do.”
“I should be going.”
Sally realized she was doing something that upset Barney. Dober was a person he recognized as a friend. He didn’t like that something he said was going to cause his friend to be disturbed. She felt bad that it had to be that way. She also felt bad that it essentially ended their conversation. Barney was no longer in the mood to share time with her.
“Yeah, I should get to work.”
“Well, don’t take so long to decide to call me next time. I’m here if you need someone to talk to.”
“Yeah, I will keep that in mind.”
“Okay, I’ll let you know if I feel we need to go any further than a conversation with Dober.”
Barney just shook his head. He was too preoccupied with the idea that Sally was going to find Dober.
Sally went to her vehicle and climbed in. She started the engine and rolled the window down. 
“Hope to see you again soon.”
Barney gave a forced smile and waved, and then hurriedly moved out of sight.
Sally left, hoping she had not ruined the chances of spending more time with a man she had feelings for since she was a little girl.
 
*****


        “Dorian, do you realize how hard your mother and I work to provide you with all the best? Young man, it’s time you started to step up. This is your senior year at Memorial. You are graduating in four months. It’s time you start acting like a young adult and not the spoiled brat you’ve done such a great job in perfecting. I am sick and tired of buying you out of trouble.”
“You are tired of buying me out of trouble, that’s a joke! You, with your high-priced lawyers that keep you out of jail for tax evasion and cheating your employees and business partners. You aren’t even faithful to Mom. I’m just like you, Daddy. All you do for me is based on money. I don’t know why you and that lady who says she is my mother ever had me. She didn’t even show up for my birthday. The two of you don’t want to spend any time with me, but that’s okay. I don’t need you, I just need what you can buy.
