Chapter 11


        The man who was once called Irish sat on the top step of the third floor stairs of the main building at the Lockwood. He was breathing heavily. He was feeling hunted and alone. There were deputies here. They were now looking for him. Their eyes had seen him. He wasn’t sure why he ran. He should have ended it there when he had the chance. That anger he carried every day, all day, was driving him. They did not belong here any more than those ghost hunter people. If people would just leave him alone. Leave this place alone, he could let that anger calm down, and go back to just being alone. 
He had been alone most of his life. When no one wanted him, he turned inward. He comforted himself, even when he was being whipped for the smallest reason. That had hardened him and closed him off in anger. He dealt with that anger by bottling it up and hiding it deep inside. It was hidden from everyone. At times, it was even hidden from himself.  
Now, as he sat there in the Lockwood, he could feel the souls of the building swirling around him. They spoke to him, calling him by name. They knew him. As they spoke, they reminded him of all the pain he had suffered. It seemed to open parts of his mind that he had kept locked up. Memories that had been stowed away, hiding from his awareness, burst into his consciousness.   
Those memories floated in and out of his mind. The anger he felt toward Mr. John the day he destroyed poor Calico, the wasted hours he had spent working as slave labor for parents who had no feelings for him. What had happened to the woman he loved, and the loss of his real mother to drugs? The worst loss of them all was his grandmother. She had been the one person he knew loved him. She died in that very building due to negligence and lack of appropriate supervision.
He now knew there was a part of him that handled all of the unfairness in his life. When he was wronged, that inner person stepped forward and took care of the transgression with impunity. The memories of the retribution flooded his mind. 
The twelve-year-old foster brother’s fate was a sad aspect of all that had happened. The highlight of his memory was the day he received a call at college informing him that his foster parents had been killed in a tragic explosion when their furnace kicked in for the first time that fall. There seemed to be some kind of gas leak that ignited after it had filled the basement of the home with propane, blowing the entire place off its foundation. The couple was killed while eating lunch. He envisioned the look in their eyes as they realized they were about to die. Irish smiled as he thought of the planning it had taken to accomplish that kind of timing.
His mind was all at once consumed with the images of eyes. All he could see were eyes. The eyes of Calico, when its head was twisted, bulged from its tiny skull. The eyes of his grandmother, gouged by the knitting needles. The eyes of the foster brother fishbowled in those absurd glasses. He was obsessed with those eyes. Everyone he saw seemed to be staring at him, judging him like Mr. John used to do. He hated to see the eyes. They were evil and mocking orbs that haunted his mind.
Blame is an interesting word. A single syllable that carries so much weight. Who was to blame for the life he had led? Was it the father who never bothered to know him? Perhaps the mother who loved drugs far more than her own child. He could blame the driver of the car that hit his grandmother. That accident took her leg and left her unable to care for him. There was plenty of cause to blame Mr. John and Ms. Wendy. Their greed and coldness consumed his formative years. He could spread blame around easily, but the Lockwood killed his grandmother. 
She was the one person who loved him unconditionally. He remembered the way she looked at him. Those were the eyes that showed love, joy, and pride. It was those eyes that he longed to see again. He had lost her twice. Once, because she could no longer take care of him, and again because no one took care of her. Those loving eyes were destroyed when she was shoved out of her chair at the Lockwood. Those eyes were gouged out of her head, and she died a painful, horrible death.
He had harbored contempt for the Lockwood for his entire life. When he heard that people were going to be coming to the place to try to interact with the spirits, he was angry. How dare someone disturb his grandmother’s rest! He had dealt with them. Those eyes had no business seeing the place.  
Now there were the cops. Eyes that were searching for him. He had to get rid of those eyes. Now that the puzzle had become complete, he knew why he was there, why he had done what he had, and why he had to continue.  He had to get rid of those probing eyes. They were hunting him, and it was him or them. He was going to finish this. He would get them. He stood up and began to descend the staircase. He began singing.


“Raccoon sleeps in a hollow tree
While the sun shines on you and me.
Sleep, little raccoon,
Sleep, little raccoon,
Warm and cozily.


He stopped singing, but he continued to hum the tune as he walked.


Hmmm, hmmm
Hmmm, hmmm


*****
        “Sally, the guy that attacked us, could that have been Barney Doyle?”
Sally hated the question for a couple of reasons. First, she was tired of Bernie’s constant accusations that Barney could be the killer, second, she could not rule Barney out based on the size and shape of the figure, and third, whoever it was had cut her; Barney wouldn’t do that! Or, would he? She stopped pondering and answered.
“No … no, why would Barney cut me?” 
“Sally, you’re bleeding!”
“What? Oh, I guess when he swung the knife as he ran past, he must have cut my arm. It will be fine.”
“No, it won’t. We need to take care of that. This place is filled with who knows what. You could get an infection. Besides that looks pretty deep. You’re losing a lot of blood.
“Oh, you’re just overreacting. We saw the guy. We need to find him.”
“You won’t be doing anyone any good if you pass out from losing blood. We need to at least get you patched up. Come on, there is a first aid kit in the cruiser.”
“This is ridiculous. We have him. We need to pursue now.”
“You said you don’t know if he went up or down. How are you going to pursue him? You don’t know what direction to go.”
“But … ”
“I’m not taking no for an answer. Come on.”
Bernie took Sally by the arm, holding the bleeding arm up as they walked. When they neared the “Grand Central” end of the second floor west wing hallway, they heard a noise in front of them. Bernie immediately pulled out her sidearm. Sally attempted to, but her arm did not respond. She reached around with her off hand and grabbed the handgun.
“Who is up there?”
There was no answer.
“This is Deputy Hart with the Eau Claire County Sheriff’s office. Show yourself now!”
There was no response. 
“You have been warned. We will use force if you do not comply.”
There was no reply. The two deputies moved forward cautiously. They had allowed him to escape once; they were determined to succeed in capturing him on this second try.
“This place will be surrounded by officers very soon. You should give yourself up. You don’t have a chance of getting away.”
The deputies kept their flashlights aimed in front of them, expecting to see someone at any moment. They walked in tandem, creeping along. Sally was dripping blood with each step. She was starting to feel the effects of no food, no rest, and now blood loss. She was fading. Bernie was not noticing as her gaze was on the hallway ahead. Sally stumbled and pulled at Bernie as she tried to right herself.
“Are you okay, Sally?”
“I’m feeling a little lightheaded right now. I just need to stop for a second and catch my breath. It’s been a very long day.”
“Are you going to be able to walk down the stairs? I don’t think I can carry you.”
“Sure … sure I will. I’m not letting a little scratch put me down.”
“Okay, well, stay behind me.”
“What about these other people?”
“What are you talking about?
“These other people, you know, the nurses and orderlies, and I guess those are patients.”
Sally pointed all around the deputies.
“I don’t understand. Do you think you see other people here?”
“What do you mean ‘think’? The people are right here; stop pretending you don’t see them. I don’t know where they came from, but they are here. I can see them with my own eyes.”
Sally wobbled a little and went over to lean on the wall.
“Sally, we need help. We are stuck on the second floor of this crazy place, and there is a killer between us and the front door. Now you’re seeing things. I’m going to try our walkie to see if Hank can hear me.”
Sally was a little out of it as she talked. 
“I don’t know why you don’t use the telephone over there.”
She pointed at the wall where there was an old mounting plate for a phone that was long gone.
Bernie just shook her head and started to call Hank on the walkie.
“This is Deputy Hart. Hank, if you can hear this, pick up.”
There was nothing for over a minute. Bernie was about to try again when her walkie sounded off, and a string of static came over the speaker. Intermingled, she heard the words, ‘repeat …  too much’. 
Bernie assumed it meant there was too much static on Hank’s end to hear them. He wanted her to try again. Bernie looked around and decided they needed to get to an outside wall to get closer to open airwaves. She grabbed Sally and threw her arm over her shoulder.
“Come on, we need to get to a window.”
 She pulled Sally along and headed down the west wing away from “Grand Central”.  She figured if they stepped into a room, they could get close to the exterior window, and the reception would be better on the walkie. Bernie wanted to get about halfway down the hall so that she would have time to make the call and then defend the door, should the killer come after them.
It took about five minutes to make their way down the hall. Bernie was perspiring by the time they made it. She leaned Sally against the wall. There was an old chair in the room, so Bernie righted it and moved her partner to the chair. The deputy had to first check the hallway to make sure no one was coming. She then went to the window. With walkie in her hand she pressed the button and began to speak.
“This is Deputy Hart for Deputy Stussie. Come in, Hank.”
“I’m … not … Hank. You … may … call … me … doctor!”
The voice that responded was obviously not her fellow deputy. It was deep and slow. It did not sound fully human.
“Who is this?’
“That … is … not … the … right … question.”
“What do you mean? This is an official Sheriff’s Office communication device. You need to clear these airwaves. You are interfering with an investigation.”
“You … are … in … my … house. You … are … interfering … in … a … place … where … you … don’t … belong!.”
“Is this Barney Doyle? Are you trying to scare me? It won’t work. Sally may not see what you have been doing, but I can.”
“Ha, ha, ha. Ha, ha, ha.”
The laugh was more like a cackle. It sent shivers down Bernie’s back. She realized she had been in the room and not watching the hallway; she moved to the door to check what was happening. Sally seemed to be talking to someone.
“Oh, it’s just a scratch. It is so nice of you to bandage it for me. Thank you. How many nurses are working here?”
Sally appeared to be looking at someone and listening.
“Oh, wow. There are that many nurses here. That’s good … that’s good.”
Sally faded out again. Bernie was beside herself. How did she let Sally rope her into doing this? She knew better. She knew that backup was called for and that they would arrive eventually. All they really needed to do was survive until they got there. As if by thinking about them she had manifested the assistance; she saw patrol lights flashing in the window. She ran to see what was happening and noticed a Dunn County Patrol vehicle pull up the driveway. She tried opening the window, but the mechanism had rusted, and it was stuck in place. She thought about breaking the window, but realized it was the old type with wire mesh embedded in the glass. Those widows are nearly unbreakable.
Bernie tried yelling to the officers who were now looking at the body by the RV. 
“Hey, we are on the second floor, west wing. There is a killer in the building!”
There was no reaction from the officers outside. Bernie grabbed her flashlight and tried to aim it toward them to get their attention, but the window was dirty, and the light barely made it out of the building. She screamed as loud as she could, but they showed no acknowledgement. Bernie stood helplessly as she watched them disappear into the building
“Sally, come on, we need to get back into the hallway.”
“No, that’s okay. I’ll stay here with Martha.”
“Who the hell is Martha?”
“She is a wonderful nurse. Do you see how well she bandaged my arm?”
Bernie could see there was no bandage, but Sally seemed calmed by the idea, so she said nothing about it. 
“Come on, we are leaving right now.”
“What, well, … ”
Bernie grabbed Sally’s arm and put it over her shoulder and tugged her to her feet. She pulled her to get her moving, and Sally dragged her feet, but followed her colleague as they reentered the hallway and headed for “Grand Central”. 
“We need to warn these guys that there is a killer in here. They could be walking into a trap.”
“Why don’t we ask these people to warn them?”
“Fine, you do that.”
Bernie was in full knowledge that Sally was too far gone to be of any assistance if they ran into the hooded figure as they made their way to the central staircase. She had a flashlight in her hand, she had Sally's waist, and she had a gun in her other hand. It was going to be a struggle if she had to hold off the killer. Her heart was pounding as she pulled Sally ever closer to the central area. She heard two shots ring out from the grand entry. Then there was no noise.
“Oh no! What has happened? Did the deputies get him?”
“That was a loud noise; it scared everyone.”
“Still not with the real world, are you, Sally?”
Sally didn’t respond; she just seemed to be in her own world, or maybe the world of the Lockwood would be a better description.
“The next move is all on me since Sally is not thinking straight.. I don’t think I can get her down the steps alone. I’ll get her to the center, and we should be able to communicate with the deputies if they are there.”
Bernie had a terrible feeling that the officers were no longer available to help. The fact that it went quiet after the gunshots made her think that something happened to them. Otherwise, they would be calling out or making noise of some kind. She needed to keep moving and face whatever had happened.
Deputy Hart had Sally and the flashlight with her sidearm at the ready. She started moving toward the open area in the central building. There she could overlook the grand entry. From that viewpoint, she should know what went down with the shots. 
Step by step they progressed to the door frame that opened into the area above the grand entry. It was only about ten feet away. Just three more steps and she would be at the top of the steps to the first floor. Just through the arched door frame, and they would be there.
As Bernie stepped through the arch, she was met by a hooded figure. Bernie screamed and let go of Sally with her handgun held straight out. Her training took over, and she yelled orders.
“Get down, face down on the floor, now! On the ground, on the ground!”
The figure lay on the ground immediately.
“Take your hood off. What is your name?”
“It’s me, Barney Doyle.”
“Barney, oh thank god you’re alive.”
Sally crawled from where Bernie dropped her to Barney. She rolled him over and hugged him.
“Sally? Are you okay? What are you doing here?”
Bernie interrupted the reunion.
“Get back on your stomach. Don’t act surprised to see us. You knew we were here. You attacked us just about an hour ago.”
“I didn’t attack you. What are you talking about?”
“You know what I’m talking about, don’t try to play dumb.”
“Whatever you are saying I did, I probably did it. I just didn’t know I was doing it.”
“Oh my god, does this place have everyone talking stupid. What do you mean you did, but you don’t know? That sounds like double-talk to me. If you don’t have the integrity to admit what you’ve done, you’re not worth speaking with.”
“I’ve seen what happened, the dead bodies, everything. I must have done it, but I don’t remember doing anything. I’m sorry, I’m sorry. I don’t know why I’m doing these things.”
Barney started to cry. Sally was trying to comfort him. Bernie was dumbfounded by the confession/nonconfession. Did Barney think he could be determined too fragile mentally to stand trial and be institutionalized instead of going to prison?
“That’s enough of the babble. Stand up. You are going to help Sally down these stairs. If you do anything, even so much as a flinch, I don’t like, I am putting you down. I’m not taking any chances, either. I will shoot to kill!” 
That statement seemed to shake Sally out of her state of delirium.
“Bernie, don’t you dare shoot him. He didn’t do these things. It’s this building; it has him talking out of his head.”
“Look who is now recognizing someone is talking out of their head. You’ve been loony for the last hour. I’m not taking a chance that I’m going to be another one of his casualties. My eyes are very clear, and they are going to stay that way right here in my head.”
They moved down the steps slowly. Bernie’s focus was solidly on the couple in front of her. None of them noticed the figure watching from the floor above. Just as the two deputies and Barney were about three steps from the first floor, the figure watching them moved toward the stairs and began a stealthy descent toward them. 
“Okay, now that we have Sally down here, get back on the floor with your hands behind your back.”
Barney obeyed Bernie’s orders. The deputy pulled handcuffs from her belt. And knelt on Barney while she locked them on his wrists. She then stood up and took a breath. She felt comfortable that she now had the killer in custody and they were safe on the first floor.
“Barney, we will get you a good lawyer. I know in my heart you could not have done this.”
“Sally, you have always been good to me. Who knows if I had not been so shy to tell you years ago how I felt, we could have been together, and my life would be so much different. That didn’t happen, and too many things happened that have tainted my view of the world and of people. That must be what happened. I lose time, and I don’t know what I do for hours at a time. No one else was here. It was just the Paranormal Posse and me. Now they are dead, and I am alive. It doesn’t take a rocket scientist to figure out who did this.”
Barney started to nod his head around the building as he said, “... figure out who did this”, and he saw the crumpled body by the door.
“Oh god! There is another body.”
The two women looked around the grand entry.
“There are two bodies.”
Bernie was walking toward the second one that was near the bottom of the second stairwell on the other side of the entry area. She rolled the body over and was presented with the gouged out eye sockets of one of the Dunn County deputies. She ran to the second one, but found he had been subjected to the type of mutilation. The deputy fell back in rage and disgust. Her stomach churned as she reflected on the terrible scene that unfolded before her. Bernie looked at Barney, the sight of him angered the deputy.
“Stop it! Why couldn’t you have let these two men live? Two more families will have the worst day of their lives because of you! Where are the kids? Have you already mutilated those poor kids? I don’t care how misguided they were; they did not deserve to die at the hands of the likes of you.”
Barney was confused. “I don’t know who you are talking about. I may have done what you say, but I don’t know anything about kids.”
Sally had been seated on the bottom step. With great effort, she rose and stumbled to the center of the entry. There was something there. The Dunn County Deputies had parked at an angle and pointed their headlights at the front of the Lockwood. The small stream of light cast a dim glow on the entry. Sally saw something reflecting the light on the floor in the center of the entry. When she managed to get to it, she saw that it was a small puddle of blood. There were drops of blood leading away from it up the other staircase opposite the one they came down. 
Sally stood staring at the blood trail and spoke softly.
“Barney, are you bleeding?”
Bernie looked at her, still angry over what she believed the demented human standing there with them had done. She spoke sharply to her fellow officer. She was now angry with her for not being willing to consider Barney a suspect to begin with.
“Are you talking to your invisible friends again? This whole mess may have been lessened if you could see beyond your feelings for this monster!”
Sally turned, ignoring Bernie’s angry outburst, and spoke to Barney.
“Barney, I asked you, are you bleeding?”
“Bleeding? No, I am fine. Other than the fact that I am obviously criminally insane.”
“I said stop with the insanity crap. You are going to pay for these killings. You are not going to spend your days being coddled and given therapy. You killed, and you will pay.”
“He didn’t kill these officers!”
“I’m tired of your belief in this guy. Give it up, of course, he killed them. He admitted it!”
“No, he said he must have because he is the only one here. If he isn’t bleeding, then whose blood is this, and why does it lead up the other stairs?”
“What are you talking about?”
“Right here. There is a puddle of blood, and look, it trails up those stairs.”
Bernie stood looking at the blood. It didn’t make sense. Whose blood would it be? They had heard the gunshots. The deputies had to have wounded someone. She needed to check Barney; maybe he was hiding the injury.
“Stand up! I need to check you for injuries. She pulled up his sweatshirt, turned him around, and frisked him top to bottom.”
“I would tell you if I were injured. I have not been shot.”
“I need to check the deputies' sidearms. I will be able to tell if they fired them.”
Bernie checked the officer by the front door first. His gun was on the floor about six feet in front of him. She pulled a pencil from her pocket and picked the weapon up by the trigger frame. She lifted it to her nose to smell and then looked into the barrel for carbon residue. 
“This one was not fired. I’ll check the second one.”
The second deputy’s gun was sitting under his right hand. The Glock 19 was a little tougher to pick up without disturbing evidence. Bernie was able to drag it from under the officer’s hand. She lifted it and did not need to get it very close to her nose to smell the sweet charcoal odor. 
“This one was fired. It adds up that someone was wounded before they killed the deputies. I suppose it could be that there was more than one.”
Sally was weak, and she had a hard time following everything that was going on. When Bernie said there could have been more than one, she immediately thought about Dorian.
“Wait, what if Ben is working with Dorian? Maybe I had it all wrong. I guess I have more than once on this case. If they were working together, then one of them could have been shot while the other attacked the deputies.”
“Yeah, that could be what happened, but I am skeptical. Two teens overpowering two trained law enforcement seems far-fetched.”
“I would have to agree. After hearing you say it, I don’t see that being what happened. Is it possible there is someone we haven’t even considered?”
“I think that is a distinct possibility. We are not going to be figuring that out now. We are going to take Barney and leave this building. I’m done playing in the dark. We're getting out of here. When the reinforcements get here, they can wrap up whatever is going on here.”
“We still haven’t found the kids. We need to check the roof.”
“Sally, there is no way you can get to the roof, or that I am staying in this hellhole any longer.”
“I can’t …”
Before Sally could protest any further there was a loud noise at the front door. The glass of the door was covered by the shadow of a figure. The rattle of a chain could be heard. Bernie ran toward the door.
“No, no, no. Who is out there? Take that chain off of the door. This is Deputy Hart of the Eau Claire County Sheriff’s Department. Step away from that door!”
Bernie drew her sidearm and aimed at the door. The shadow remained, filling the door. Bernie fired her handgun. The shadow did not move. She fired again, and the shadow seemed to walk through the wall and stood looming over the deputy.
“What … what … wha … do you see this?”
Sally was leaning against the staircase banister, staring at the huge shadow now filling half of the grand entry. Then the shadow started to laugh. It was a maniacal laugh. The deep voice was the embodiment of evil. It shook Sally and Bernie. Barney had dealt with the shadow many times. 
“Leave them alone. I know you don’t want me to leave. I don’t matter, but leave these women alone.”
“Barney, have you seen this thing before?”
“Yes, I tried to believe it was all in my head. But the Lockwood wants me to stay and become one of them. This shadow has followed me since I arrived here. I don’t know what it is, but it pulls me in and connects to the darkness that hides within me. I believe it is that darkness that kills. I want it to end, I hate that part of me.”
Sally stumbled to Barney’s side.
“Barney, you can’t become part of this place. The shadow is not the Lockwood. It is feared and hated by the residents. I know the truth; the nurses told me. They have been talking to me.”
Bernie could not believe what they were saying or what she was seeing. This was all too much like a bad dream.
“Ha ha ha. You will all be part of my happy troop of spirits. You will not be leaving here. My friend will soon see to that.”
“What is he talking about?” 
“I think he means me, Sally. That is what I’ve been saying. I’m the one doing these horrible things. He is making me do it. He has connected with the darkness inside me. I can’t help myself. I don’t know when I’m doing these killings, and I don’t remember them. I’m no good. I deserve to stay here, but you don’t. He thinks he can make me kill you. I hope I can resist his power over me. Keep my hands in these cuffs; please, don’t let him free me.”
Barney was nearing hysteria. He was sure the friend the shadow was referring to was him. Bernie believed Barney was correct. She grabbed for her sidearm. 
“Barney, this thing can’t make you do something if you’re dead. I can end this.”
Sally saw the gun and what Bernie intended to do. She could not let her kill the man she loved, no matter what he may have done.
“No, don’t do it!”
Sally summoned the last of her energy and charged her colleague. The two of them tumbled to the ground, and the gun slid across the marble floor to the other side of the entry. Bernie lunged to grab it, with Sally hanging on with all of the strength she could muster. The two wrestled on the floor until they stopped when a strange chanting started.


“Raccoon sleeps in a hollow tree
While the sun shines on you and me.
Sleep, little raccoon,
Sleep, little raccoon,
Warm and cozily.


In the darkest part of night
Raccoon has the best eyesight.
Look, little raccoon,
Look, little raccoon,
My, your eyes are bright.


Raccoon hardly makes a sound
When he prowls all around.
Hunt, little raccoon,
Hunt, little raccoon,
Find food on the ground.”


        Lying on the floor, the deputies looked toward the voice emanating from the floor above them. They watched as a hooded figure began to descend the stairs, singing/chanting the children's rhyme. The figure walked with a limp. The lower right pant leg was soaked with blood. Sally scanned from the wounded leg up to the torso. That sweatshirt, she had seen it before.
        The shadow spoke again. The glee in its voice dripped with contempt for the living. 
        “Here is my friend, we’ve known each other for a long time now, haven’t we, Charley?”
        “Yes, we have. I’ve provided you with several new toys today. Now I will finish this.” 
He raised his hand, and the long, thin knife glinted in the dim light of the flashlights now lying about the floor. He walked slowly, relishing his final kills. He stood over the deputies as if he were deciding which melon to buy at the grocery store. He grabbed Bernie and swung back with the knife.
“Stop! Charley, what are you doing? I taught you better than that, young man!”
“Grandma? Is that you? It is you! Oh, Grandma, I missed you.”
“My poor boy, I’m sorry I had to let you go. I couldn’t take care of you anymore. Then the accident, and I was gone.”
“I’ve been here every day hoping to see you. All I ever saw was him. He said if I did these things, he would bring me to you. I had to see you again. You were the only person who ever really loved me.” 
“Charley, you shouldn’t have listened to him. He is evil. He killed those nurses, and he paid the price in the boiler room. Now he sucks the evil from those who enter here. You have made him stronger by doing those horrible things. You should have known better. You are Charley Cochran. Let me see your face.”  
The man pulled the hood down so that the spirit of his grandmother could see him once more.
Sally looked at the man in shock.
“Doc? Doc Blanchard? Was it you all this time? D O, Doc! Fred knew it was you! You did all of this. How could you? The people here trusted you.”
Bernie pulled away from the grip Blanchard had on her and ran to her gun on the floor. She held it on Blanchard as he watched his grandmother’s spirit fade away. 
That shadow still wanted blood. It ordered Doc to act.
“Kill them! If you want to see her again, you will kill them!”
Blanchard, the boy who grew up as Irish, sat down on the floor. Tears filled his eyes, and he started to sing the song his grandmother taught him so many years ago.


“Raccoon sleeps in a hollow tree
While the sun shines on you and me.
Sleep, little raccoon,
Sleep, little raccoon,
Warm and cozily.


In the darkest part of night
Raccoon has the best eyesight.
Look, little raccoon,
Look, little raccoon,
My, your eyes are bright.


Raccoon hardly makes a sound
When he prowls all around.
Hunt, little raccoon …….


The man’s voice trailed off as he turned inward and went silent. The two deputies and Barney were stunned at what just happened. The shadow was still looming over them all. No one moved as they processed what they had seen.        
The shadow was enraged and focused his attention on Barney. 
“You! You wanted to stay here with me. I can make that happen; you can live here forever. Be my sword, do my killing, and I can make that happen.”
Barney froze as he felt singled out by the shadow that had tormented him. Sally made her way to him.
“Don’t listen to it. It does not control you. You are better than that. I need you. I … I … I love you.”
Barney looked at her, and his heart filled with joy and optimism. That was something he had not experienced for most of his life. It felt wonderful, like rebirth. 
The shadow seemed to know it was losing and let out a howling scream that was cut short as a burst of light illuminated the room. Standing above them along the balustrade of the second floor stood a tall woman. It was the spirit of Mary Lockwood.
“You are done here. I built this hospital as a place of healing. You have turned it into a house of pain. I will no longer allow it. You will leave. It is time you descend to the depths where you were destined to go.”
Sally, Bernie, and Barney watched the light from the spirit at the balcony intensify and brighten until it blinded them, forcing them to shield their eyes. When they could tell it had dimmed, they opened their eyes. Mary Lockwood had vanished, and the shadow figure was gone. Bernie was still holding the gun on Doc Blanchard. Barney was still in cuffs, and Sally was nearly passed out, sitting at his feet. They were trying to understand what had just happened, but they had very little time to do so. 
The front door burst open, and a flood of deputies, State Patrol Officers, and the Sheriff charged into the grand entry. 
Once the situation was sorted out, Bernie, Sally, and Barney stood in front of the building just enjoying the chance to breathe the fresh air. They could now reflect on the nightmare they had just experienced. The sun was just starting to rise, and the dim light of early morning shone on the Lockwood, casting long shadows on the landscape beyond.
